#2672. Eagles notebook: Nolan Smith reacts to the Jordan Davis trade

Eagles notebook: Nolan Smith reacts to the Jordan Davis trade originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Nolan Smith and Jordan Davis were teammates at Georgia and then became teammates again in the NFL. They have been friends for a long time.

"Man, he's a great guy," Smith said on Friday. "That's part of the business. That's how it goes. I'm pretty sure Jacksonville is picking up a good player. But we have to focus on us. We have leaders in this locker room. We've got guys who can lead and push guys."

Davis was traded to the Jaguars ahead of the trade deadline last week and this was the first chance to catch up with Smith since the trade. So it has been over a week since it all went down. Jalen Carter, who also played with Davis at Georgia before becoming teammates in the NFL, was again not available to reporters this week.

The return on the Davis trade for the Eagles was undeniable. Getting a first-round pick and second-round pick for Davis represented incredible value. But it doesn't mean they won't miss Davis.

"Man, it was special," Smith said of their time together in Philly. "But that time come and go, man. I'm still playing with guys that I played with at Georgia. But that was a real special moment, though."

The Eagles are back from their bye week and Sean Mannion is still their offensive coordinator and play-caller. Mannion this week even said he got a vote of confidence from Nick Sirianni.

"We're in conversation all the time," Mannion said. "Nick's been totally supportive since I got here in, what was it? I guess February, January. We remain in lockstep and it's great getting to work with him every day.

"Like I said, it's the same things I'm saying about the joy I get working with the players in here. The same can be said about our coaching staff, whether it be with Nick, with our assistants, everybody across the board, it's been totally positive."

If Sirianni decided to hand over play-calling to a different coach during the bye week, it would have been understandable. After all, the Eagles are 32nd out of 32 teams in yards this season and are 30th in points. But this is Mannion's offense and going to someone else (like Josh Grizzard) likely wouldn't fix the problems.

Mannion, 34, is new at this. It's his first year as an NFL offensive coordinator and his first time as a play caller. There was always likely going to be a learning curve; but it has been steeper than many envisioned.

But the fact that Sirianni has been asked multiple times this season if he's sticking with Mannion is telling. A lot of the outside criticisms of Mannion are warranted. Mannion tries to block out that discussion.

"I don't pay attention to any of that stuff," Mannion said. "I'm just totally focused on the role I'm in and doing everything I possibly can to help our players be successful, help our team be successful. You mentioned it, but just kind of blocking out the outside noise. I'm doing everything I can every day to help the Eagles and to help us win and be a successful offense. If there's things that are kind of orbiting outside that, it doesn't pertain to that task and that focus that I have on what's going on here."

Eagles quarterback Joe Milton is known for his strong right arm, but he's not a fan of a nickname that has been attacked to him since college: Bazooka Joe.

He prefers the nickname Mr. Rocket, "because bazookas only travel so far."

With this in mind, let's take a look at some nicknames for other Eagles players in 2026. We got some help from ProFootballReference and the Eagles' media guide for this:

The Eagles late last week waived veteran tackle Fred Johnson, who played an important role with the team for several years.

From 2023-26, Johnson played in 58 games with 14 starts. He resurrected his career in Philly, which led to this unforgettable moment in the 2024 season when Johnson had to enter the game for Lane Johnson in a 15-12 win in the Superdome.

This was Johnson's first real playing time since the 2021 season. And all of his teammates were thrilled for him.

Everybody knows some of my story. It's just been a long time coming. When I seen him running to score, I got hype, real hype. I was just grateful for the moment. I'm ready to go back out and do it again."

"I talk about it a lot on Fridays in our meetings right here. Something very similar to last year with Detroit. We knew (Dave) Fipp and Detroit ran a lot of fakes in that 4th-and-2, 4th-and-3 area, but it's just my job to give these guys the information. Hopefully, they can retain it, and a lot of the time they do, so they keep themselves, one, from getting hurt and, two, alert for something, a team trying to steal an extra possession, especially in a defensive back-and-forth."

#2671. Daniel Jones clears waivers, becomes free agent

As expected, former Colts quarterback Daniel Jones has cleared waivers. He’s now a free agent.

The same thing happened two years ago, after the Giants benched Jones for the same financial reasons (i.e., to avoid the vesting of injury guarantees) and then released him at his request.

Two years ago, Jones eventually joined the Vikings’ practice squad. And joining a practice squad seems like a viable option again, unless he prefers to remain truly free and clear until the planets perfectly align for him to join a contender and start playing.

He’ll continue to be paid by the Colts because his full 2026 salary is guaranteed. Given the rollercoaster ride he has experienced this year in Indianapolis, at this point he may just want to take a break.

Still, as we’ve seen time and again, there aren’t enough good quarterbacks to go around. Even if it’s debatable, based on his performance in 2026, whether he remains a “good quarterback.”

#2670. Rapid Recap: Bucks 116, Raptors 111

The Milwaukee Bucks won—yes, won!—their second game of the season in a back-and-forth, tense matchup against the host Toronto Raptors. The Bucks rode a fourth-quarter surge to cement a commanding lead over the Raptors that they just couldn't overcome. Tyler Herro scored 32 points, and Myles Turner posted a 27-point, 11-rebound double-double. Scottie Barnes led the Raptors with 31 points.

Jakob Poeltl scored the game's first points when he grabbed an offensive board and muscled off Kyle Kuzma to convert the and-one. The Raptors quickly jumped out to a 14-4 lead, helped by hot three-point shooting, but the Bucks responded by scoring seven straight points to cut Toronto's lead to four. Jaime Jaquez Jr. helped the Bucks take the lead with a three-pointer with four minutes remaining, but that was by no means permanent. After Collin Murray-Boyles and Tyler Herro missed back-to-back dunks, the Bucks and Raptors traded leads before Brayden Burries tied it up at 28 with a pull-up three at the end of the first.

Both sides reverted to their usual offensive selves at the start of the second: clunky and inefficient in the half-court. Jamal Shead, who shoots south of 28% from three this season, connected on a three to push Toronto's lead to 33-31, but Myles Turner immediately fired back a triple to preserve the Bucks' one-point edge. Because of each side's offensive struggles—or, depending on how you look at it, defensive success—the lead remained small for both sides. Turner then regained the flow he had in the first quarter, clicking from long range and preying on Toronto's lack of interior size. Toronto held an edge to close the half, with buckets from Murray-Boyles and Immanuel Quickley extending their edge to four, 53-49, at the long break.

The game remained tight to open the second half, with Turner continuing his hot shooting, splashing a three to tie it at 55, his fourth bomb of the game. However, Toronto built a seven-point lead with Poeltl's and-one layup as the Raptors found success getting to the line and drawing fouls, especially on Turner. That, along with Barnes' high-flying athleticism and midrange prowess, helped Toronto build a slight buffer. However, it wasn't enough, as the Bucks came roaring back; Herro's floater gave the visitors a 73-72 lead, and although Jamison Battle quickly retook it with a pull-up three, it felt like the Raptors wouldn't easily make it out of Scotiabank Arena with a win. Nate Ament took the lead on a driving layup with 2:07 to go, and the Raptors struggled to respond, largely because of their second-half shooting struggles. Kel'el Ware jammed home a putback dunk in the waning seconds, giving the Bucks an 85-80 lead at the end of the third.

The momentum began to shift towards the hosts as the fourth got going. Allen Graves knocked down a wing three, cutting Milwaukee's edge down to one, and the Raptors took the lead when Barnes found Poeltl for a short floater. Although the Bucks went on a long field goal drought, they got into the bonus really early in the quarter, which let them keep their noses in front. That said, Ja'Kobe Walter lit Scotiabank Arena on fire when he hit a long three to tie the game at 98, but Kuzma immediately responded with a triple of his own to quiet them back down. Turner then went on a personal 6-0 run with a wing triple and an and-one on Poeltl, fouling him out of the game—huge! The Raptors couldn't overcome that lead, even as Turner fouled out as well. The Bucks kept hitting big shots, including Burries' acrobatic layup and a dagger Herro jumper, while Toronto flatlined on offense. Milwaukee ends its 13-game losing streak!

The Bucks had their best shooting night of the season so far, making 14 three-pointers on 50% shooting. Coincidentally, this game also saw the fewest three-point shots (28) the Bucks have attempted all season, highlighting the Bucks' paint dominance over Toronto's frontcourt.

Myles Turner has firmly put the starting center conversation to rest today. Despite the fouling concerns and turnover issues, Turner looked like the stretch five Milwaukee dreamed of when they paid him $106 million over four years. He gave the Bucks elite spacing in the half-court offense, letting slashing guards like Herro and Burries feast inside. He was also an imposing interior defender, and his two blocks and constant vertical challenges won't be ignored.

#2669. 2 blockbuster trades Pacers must target to save season after Tyrese Haliburton injury ruins start

2 blockbuster trades Pacers must target to save season after Tyrese Haliburton injury ruins start appeared first on ClutchPoints. Add ClutchPoints as a Preferred Source by clicking here.

The Indiana Pacers entered the 2026-27 season with the hope that their nightmare 2025-26 campaign was nothing more than a one-off, as Tyrese Haliburton was set to return from his year-long absence after rupturing his Achilles in the biggest game of his life — Game 7 of the 2025 NBA Finals against the Oklahoma City Thunder.

Haliburton did look healthy to start the new campaign, averaging 21.8 points and 9.5 assists in four games. However, after playing nearly 35 minutes in a 110-102 loss to the Milwaukee Bucks back on the 26th of October, reports came out that the Pacers star had suffered a strained hamstring, sidelining him for weeks.

To this day, Haliburton remains out with this injury, and it's unclear as to when he'll be making his return to the hardwood. Even after earning a 125-120 victory over the injury-ravaged Thunder on Monday night, they are only 6-12 on the season — good for just 11th place in the Eastern Conference.

Watch sports LIVE with fuboTV (free trial)

There is a long way to go in the season, and as they showed during the 2024-25 season, they can turn things around when they get a clean bill of health as a team. They started that season with a 6-10 record through 16 games, but still finished with a 50-win campaign.

But considering the question marks regarding Haliburton's health, it may not be the worst idea for the Pacers to not just strengthen the team, but also insure it in the event of another injury to their superstar floor general.

Here are two blockbuster trade ideas the Pacers have to entertain to rescue themselves from a brutal start.

Pacers trade: TJ McConnell, Jarace Walker, Ben Sheppard, Jay Huff, 2027 IND first-round pick, 2028 IND first-round pick swap, 2030 IND first-round pick

Trey Murphy III is expected to command plenty of interest on the trade market yet again even as the New Orleans Pelicans remain stubborn with making their current core work. The problem for the Pelicans is that the whole league knows of their dysfunction, and amid the team's instability, some of their players' performances have dropped off dramatically — with Murphy suffering one of the biggest drop-offs on the team.

Murphy scored just nine points in 35 minutes of play in the Pelicans' overtime loss to the Phoenix Suns on Monday, and this has been a continuation of a worrying trend for the 26-year-old forward.

He's averaging just 15.1 points per game this season, down from 21.5 last season, and his shooting splits have gotten worse across the board. Moreover, he's missed eight games already for New Orleans this season due to a knee injury.

Murphy's decline in production stems from his confusing role in the Pelicans' disorganized offense. It almost seems like their offense runs on vibes, and Murphy has gotten lost in the shuffle as a result — averaging over four fewer shots per contest than he did last season.

A change of scenery appears to be necessary for Murphy, and it's not too difficult to imagine just how much he'll thrive in this Pacers team — especially when Haliburton is healthy. Murphy can create his own shot, but he thrives when others set the table for him, and that's exactly what Haliburton will do.

Nearly 77% of Murphy's made field-goals are assisted, and with a dynamic playmaker like Haliburton in charge of the Pacers' offense, he should receive a boost in production in Indianapolis should the Pacers manage to actually trade for him.

The problem is that the Pelicans will once again be demanding the world in trade talks involving the 26-year-old. They were reportedly looking for a package similar to what the Brooklyn Nets and Memphis Grizzlies received for Mikal Bridges and Desmond Bane, respectively.

But New Orleans is not in a position of leverage, and the Pacers would do well to acquire Murphy without giving up Aaron Nesmith, a playoff hero in 2025, and Obi Toppin, an important rotation player. TJ McConnell will be missed, but he's been injured for most of this season anyway, and the team has Braden Smith and Quenton Jackson as capable guards off the bench.

They did give up two first-round picks in a trade for Ivica Zubac, so they will be hesitant to give away more draft assets. But for a player like Murphy, forking over those picks could very well be worth the risk.

Pacers trade: TJ McConnell, Jarace Walker, Ben Sheppard, top-12 protected 2027 first-round pick

Offense has been hard to come by for the Pacers amid Haliburton's injury. Through 18 games, they rank 21st in offensive rating, scoring just 111.3 points per 100 possessions.

While it's easy for fans and analysts to urge the Pacers to make a blockbuster trade, they have to keep in mind that they're building a roster around Haliburton. This means that they simply cannot just trade players for the sake of doing so.

Some would put Aaron Nesmith, Obi Toppin, and even Andrew Nembhard on the trade machine. But these three players are essential to Indiana's identity as a fast-paced team that can wreak havoc on defense as well. McConnell is also important, but there's a chance that he may never be sustainably healthy again.

But as was mentioned earlier, the Pacers need more of a scoring punch, and the player they need to seek out on the trade market has to be a good fit alongside the team's other core pieces as well. To that end, someone like Naz Reid could be a good get.

Reid has struggled mightily with the Charlotte Hornets, as his shooting efficiency has tanked completely amid that team's offensive woes. He is averaging 17.7 points per contest, but he's also shooting just 38.2% from the field, which is suboptimal to say the least.

Playing alongside Haliburton should once again help out Reid, and he gives the Pacers a go-to-guy for when Haliburton and Siakam need to catch a breather. He could also be more of a paint-bound defender, as Toppin and Siakam have the mobility to cover the more perimeter-oriented matchups.

Having Reid should also give the Pacers added matchup flexibility. In teams that can exploit Zubac's lack of perimeter mobility, Reid could be the man they go to when they need a bruising interior presence on the floor all while maintaining respectable floor spacing.

If Indiana can get Reid for the low price of a heavily-protected first-round pick, they should be all over that trade in a heartbeat. It's likely that the Hornets end up keeping Reid, but if Charlotte keeps on losing games, they have to at least think about trading him away.

#2668. Top Wall Street analysts favor these 3 dividend stocks for strong returns

Investors often favor dividend stocks for their regular income and the promise of potential share price appreciation. But selecting the right dividend stocks from a huge pool of companies can be difficult.

Top Wall Street analysts can help shortlist attractive dividend-paying stocks, as their recommendations are backed by thorough analysis of a company's financials.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Devon Energy (DVN), an independent oil and natural gas exploration and production company, reported better-than-expected third-quarter results. The company announced a quarterly dividend of 32 cents per share for the fourth quarter, payable December 30. Based on the fixed dividends paid over the trailing 12 months, DVN yields 2.85%.

After hosting a virtual investor meeting with management, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on Devon, with a price target of $59. Mehta said that he is now more confident in management's outlook for delivering strong near-term capital returns as well as efforts to drive operational efficiencies and productivity gains.

Devon sees an opportunity to increase share repurchases, driven by solid free cash flow (FCF), contributions from the Eagle Ford divestiture and continued improvement in the balance sheet, Mehta said. Devon expects share buybacks worth more than $1 billion in Q4 2026, up from $414 million in the third quarter, backed by a strong pricing backdrop and improved capital efficiency.

Mehta, a 5-star analyst, said Devon expects about $6.3 billion of excess FCF in 2027, including about $3.5 billion of net proceeds from the Eagle Ford sale. That would enable the company to accelerate buybacks while progressing toward its goal of reducing gross debt to about $9 billion by early 2027. Mehta noted that under DVN's framework to return up to 70% of annual FCF, management favors share buybacks as it believes the stock is discounted compared to large-cap peers.

"Management expects continued capital efficiency gains from merger synergies, extended laterals, surfactant adoption and development of the newly acquired federal acreage," Mehta said.

Mehta ranks No. 876 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 8.1%. See Devon Energy Stock Buybacks on TipRanks.

Energy Transfer (ET), is a limited partnership that operates about 140,000 miles of pipeline and associated energy infrastructure. The company recently announced third-quarter results showing net income per common unit growing about 18% year-over-year, to 33 cents. ET's cash distribution for Q3 2026 was 34.25 cents per common unit, up more than 3% year-over-year. At an annualized distribution of $1.37 per share, Energy Transfer yields more than 7%.

TD Cowen analyst Jason Gabelman reiterated a buy rating on Energy Transfer with a price target of $25. The company delivered better-than-expected Q3 results and announced the 1.2 billion cubic feet per day (bcf/d) South Mississippi Pipeline, a 215-mile, 36-inch natural gas line linking the Perryville Hub to the Florida Gas Transmission (FGT) network, Gabelman said. The project is supported by 20-year firm transportation agreements and is expected to enter service in 2030. ET expects to ultimately own 51% of the $1.6 billion project.

The 5-star analyst said that Energy Transfer lowered its 2026 capital spending outlook to $5.5 billion (midpoint of the guidance range) from $5.75 billion due to project timing. Energy Transfer expects capex to rise to $7 billion in 2027, while Gabelman projects $5 billion in both 2028 and 2029, based on disclosed projects.

Gabelman also highlighted management's commentary about optimization initiatives contributing an incremental $800 million to 2026 earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization, a larger benefit than in recent years. "A return to less commodity volatility should the [Middle East] war end could bring some relative downside, though this is offset by continued strong growth prospects," he said.

Gabelman ranks No. 397 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 15.4%. See Energy Transfer KPIs on TipRanks.

Oil and gas exploration and production company ConocoPhillips (COP) delivered better-than-expected Q3 performance. The company announced an 11% hike in its quarterly dividend to 93 cents per share, effective with the Q4 dividend payable on December 1. Based on the dividends paid over the trailing 12 months, COP yields about 2.6%.

Following the Q3 results, Wells Fargo analyst Sam Margolin repeated a buy rating on ConocoPhillips, citing strong potential for further dividend increases. Margolin also trimmed his price target to $185 from $189 to reflect timing effects and the WAHA forward curve, though he said that COP could still re-rate as its natural portfolio lifecycle provides stronger protection against inflationary pressures than peers. The Waha forward curve tracks future natural gas prices based on the West Texas Waha hub.

Margolin, another 5-star analyst, highlighted that ConocoPhillips' Q3 2026 adjusted earnings per share of $3.27 was well above Wells Fargo and the Street's estimates of $3.04 and $3.05, respectively. Earnings were driven by solid price realizations, partially offset by higher depletion, depreciation and amortization and purchased commodities.

Margolin also noted COP's dividend increase and said the accelerated buyback enabled by current commodity prices could further support long-term dividend growth. He sees the possibility of ConocoPhillips' dividend growth rate exceeding its current 6% cost of equity into the end of the decade. Margolin expects COP to sustain an 8% dividend compound annual growth rate through 2029 while repurchasing $6.4 billion of stock annually, and reducing net debt-to-capital to less than 9% by the end of 2028 based on the assumption of an average Brent crude oil price of $75 a barrel.

"The message on the 3Q call was similar to that of 2Q, reiterating the moderation in reinvestment rate and commitment to dividend growth through the Willow start-up and after," Margolin said.

Margolin ranks No. 189 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 69% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.6%. See ConocoPhillips Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

#2665. Turner reignites trade discourse after Raptors lose to Bucks

The Toronto Raptors fell to the Milwaukee Bucks 116-111 on Monday.

After a potentially season-defining win in front of a national audience against the Detroit Pistons, this loss served as quite the momentum-killer, particularly against an incredibly weak Bucks squad.

Record aside, Raptors head coach Darko Rajakovic believes the Bucks are better than their current place in the Eastern Conference

"I think they're a good team. Tyler Herro just scored 50 points the other night," said Rajakovic. "They lost on the last possession to Philly. They're an NBA team."

Here are four more takeaways from the game:

Adding Myles Turner is certainly a tired take among Raptors fans, but with the Bucks centre in town, it was an unofficial audition. If anything, it was an opportunity to erase his zero points (on seven shot attempts) and seven-rebound performance against Toronto on Nov. 6.

Turner made his presence felt right from the opening tip, scoring Milwaukee's first nine points.

By default, it also turned into a duel between Turner and Jakob Poeltl. In their meeting earlier this month, it was Poeltl who dominated, finishing with 19 points, 11 rebounds, three steals and one block. This time, Turner more than evened the score, recording 18 points and 10 rebounds by halftime.

Turner ultimately finished with 27 points and 11 rebounds on 75 per cent shooting. He also knocked down four triples on five attempts.

Beyond the box score, there was a litany of fodder for basketball pundits online. After Turner knocked down a signature three from the top of the arc, Poeltl was called for an illegal screen on the ensuing possession. Late in the half, Poeltl hesitated to take a clean look. He then drove to the rim before getting emphatically blocked by Turner. Late in the game, Turner knocked down a three on a pick-and-pop, and then blocked Poeltl again on the next possession. Going back up the court, Turner drove to the basket and drew a foul on Poeltl, sending the Raptors centre to the bench with his sixth personal foul.

As a note, Turner also fouled out moments later.

The Raptors next play the Bucks on Feb. 6, which is scheduled six days before the NBA's trade deadline. That matchup could serve as the tipping point for a potential trade. Regardless, Turner looked like an intriguing piece to complement Scottie Barnes and Kawhi Leonard, especially when it comes to spacing.

Without Leonard, the Raptors' offence must consist of a healthy dose of Barnes, supported by a balanced attack from the tertiary options. Against the Bucks, at least for most of the game, the offence was exactly that. Less than six minutes into the contest, all five starters had contributed to the Raptors' first 18 points.

"This team, the way we're playing right now, we need every single player to contribute," said Rajakovic.

In the second half, Barnes came out with an elevated aggression level. The All-Star took Toronto's first five field goal attempts to score seven points. On the team's sixth shot attempt, it was Barnes who assisted Poeltl's first basket of the half.

Barnes finished with 31 points, five rebounds, and eight assists. In his sixth year in the league, Barnes is consistently hitting a level where he's arguably the best player on the court, and he's doing so without the free-throw volume. Barnes went 5-for-6 from the charity stripe.

Barnes' 31 points also moved him into seventh for all-time Raptors scorers, passing headband magician Morris Peterson. He now sets his sights on Il Mago himself, Andrea Bargnani.

Ja'Kobe Walter has been the main beneficiary of Leonard's absence. Walter has already made 14 starts after a career-high 19 appearances in the starting lineup in 2025-26. Walter finished with 15 points while shooting 5-for-12 from the field. After dropping 25 points against the New York Knicks (Nov. 17) and 18 points vs. the Washington Wizards (Nov. 18), Walter had a forgettable 2-for-11 shooting night against the Detroit Pistons on Nov. 20.

"We're learning, we're getting better, we're improving but we're not consistent," said Rajakovic. "That's what we have to find."

An encouraging aspect of Walter's development has been his willingness to get on the glass. He finished with six rebounds, matching a total he's hit three other times this season.

On defence, he's been reliable next to Barnes. There were a few instances where he poked a ball loose as the point-of-attack defender, forcing an opposing guard to backtrack past halfcourt. Walter recorded one steal and one block. It was Walter's fourth consecutive game with at least one takeaway

Despite this, Herro finished with a game-high 32 points. Walter and the rest of the defence had contained Herro in the first half, but the Bucks guard ultimately found his rhythm.

"We held him to three points, so we're definitely capable," said Walter. "We definitely let him get comfortable, we let him get to what he does. When a good player gets confidence, it's hard to stop them."

"We need to get more stops and rebound the ball and really dominate on the defensive end so we have some leeway on the offensive side," said Rajakovic.

#2660. Report: NFL Primetime documentary is in the works

The recent passing of Tom Jackson has sparked much discussion about ESPN’s NFL Primetime, which was for years a mandatory Sunday evening destination for football fans everywhere.

As it turns out, a documentary is coming regarding the show.

Via Sam Neumann of Awful Announcing, the final product is believed to be on track for a release in connection with the Super Bowl, which is being televised by ESPN and ABC.

It could be part of the game-day programming. Three years ago, CBS debuted You Are Looking Live! — a documentary about The NFL Today — before the launch of the official pregame show in advance of Super Bowl LVIII.

For those who were actively following the NFL in the late ‘80s, NFL Primetime provided the first opportunity to see extended highlights of NFL games on the day they were played. Years before the Internet, social media, Sunday Ticket, and RedZone, Primetime became appointment viewing for fans who wanted to see more about what was happening beyond the two or three games available in their local markets on Sunday afternoon.

And the team of Chris Berman and Tom Jackson became the tour guides for the opportunity to essentially watch all of the important plays from every game in a compressed but meaningful package, with Berman’s cadence and the music accompanying the words creating the loose impression that it was all happening live. Even though everyone knew it wasn’t.

It became a unique moment in time, which at the time didn’t seem to have a shelf life. It’s just the way things were, with fans having no concept that there would ever be a way to watch all of the games live or to see any and all relevant plays long before 7:00 p.m. ET. It was a new frontier that no one had reason to believe would not remain a permanent fixture of each and every NFL Sunday.

As Neumann explains it, Jackson recently made a surprise return to ESPN as part of the filming of the documentary. ESPN shared footage of the reunion on Sunday.

It’s worth your time to watch it, if you were one of the millions who climbed aboard the Primetime Express every Sunday night of football season for something much more meaningful than a glimpse of the entire day of NFL action. And the documentary will undoubtedly bring back fond memories of the final years before advances in technology caused a narrow range of football options to explode into today’s endless menu of choices.

#2659. The biggest money-making energy trade looks over. What to buy instead

Is one of the greatest money-making trades of the last few years coming to an end?

No, I'm not talking about Nvidia or any of the data center- or AI-related power companies (although they have been crushed lately too… more on that on CNBC TV. Tune in!).

I'm talking about whether the epic boom in refinery-related stocks is unwinding. Over the last year, the jump in some of these companies has been nothing short of remarkable.

That jump is largely due to massive increases in margins, earnings, and cash flow. Those have occurred because of a big jump in what is called a crack spread. That is the difference between the price of oil and the finished price of refined products such as diesel and jet fuel. For most of this year, spreads have been soaring as disruptions and attacks on facilities and shipping in the Arabian Gulf, Hormuz, and deep inside Russia have played havoc with the market. In the last week or so, however, something big has changed: many of those spreads have started to tighten.

This seismic drop in spreads is causing a selloff in the aforementioned refinery companies.

If spreads continue to fall, these stocks may well keep selling off from recent highs. The upshot for investors may be that, as the shares come down, analysts see more upside. The median price target for Phillips 66 (PSX) now indicates a 20% gain from here, while smaller-cap Par Pacific (PARR) is double that.

Here are a few other key points to focus on heading into the Thanksgiving-shortened week.

I like to keep politics as much at arm's length as possible — we all know that energy and politics (sadly) often go hand in hand. Energy policies coming from Washington, D.C., can drive — or destroy — investment dollars. With all the big shifts in the White House over the last 10 years (four years of Trump, four years of Biden, and now 2 years into Trump #2), energy policies can blow through Washington faster than a summer squall hits a wind turbine. But because federal policies can mean so much for specific companies, sectors, and investments, it's important to follow the news flow.

That said, I'd like to take a moment to introduce you to the Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act, known as BAAJA (and pronounced like the state in Mexico). Like much legislation, BAAJA is an obscure but important bill working its way through the American Congress. BAAJA does a lot of things, but what Power Insider readers care about is its focus on energy permitting reform. In a nutshell, BAAJA would help speed up the building of new electricity infrastructure. Again, that's an overly simplistic view of a big bill, but it needs to be on your radar. The utility trade group Edison Electric Institute came out in favor of the legislation this month after the industry received some 11th-hour changes. See more about it in our recent interview with EEI President Drew Maloney here.

The bill passed the U.S. Senate in a vote late Thursday night. Now it moves to the House. Support for its final passage is also coming from the big LNG trade group, whose executive director said he "applauds the Senate for its bipartisan effort to pass meaningful, bipartisan, and durable permitting reforms aimed at delivering greater predictability, consistency, and certainty in the permitting, production, and export process for U.S. LNG. This legislation moves us closer to untangling red tape and eliminating overlapping agency reviews that can keep U.S. energy projects delayed for years."

The Natural Gas Supply Association also chimed in, saying it urges Congress to see the bill as "a resource-agnostic and balanced reform agenda for boosting American energy security. It keeps strong environmental reviews in place, but it draws a firm line against the endless procedural delays that tie critical energy projects in knots for a decade."

In my view, passing BAAJA is an important test of Congress's appetite for helping the energy and electrical power industry, lowering costs, and speeding time to market. The old adage was correct: time is money. Literally. If BAAJA moves on through the House, we could see an upside move in some utility stocks and any company related to energy infrastructure.

Speaking of politics, the news is getting lost in the massive Anthropic (ANT) IPO, but the Texas Commission on Environmental Quality (TCEQ) — whose importance I wrote about in the last edition — just decided to resume permitting for data centers. The Texas Tribune did a nice write-up on it, and you can read it here.

It's tough to say, but it's true. The two are inextricably linked by policies created at both the federal and state levels. As I wrote in the last edition, some federally elected officials may have less impact on state-level issues — like the data center buildout — but they certainly have power over national laws. While many of you are enthralled, disgusted, or confused by sordid tales of Nvidia and Jensen-gate, keep one eye open on what happens in Washington, D.C.

Have a great week, and have a wonderful Thanksgiving, everyone.

Perhaps the big investor story — which is, like most things lately, related to Jensen-gate and Nvidia—is the sizable selloff in many AI, neocloud, and data center power-related stocks.

Painful for current shareholders, but big drops can also bring big opportunities. Wall Street believes that's the case, at least right now. Look at some of the potential upside based on the stocks' prices versus their price targets.

We spoke with the CEO of Applied Digital (APLD) about all of this on CNBC last week, and you can watch it here. He highlighted how the market may be getting these stock stories wrong because, at least with his company, he has long-term contracts with solid-credit companies. In that way, Applied Digital is more like a landlord than a "pure-play" AI company.

My take → While I'm not going to recommend a specific stock, I would add that it is critical for investors to understand the differences between these companies. While they may have some things in common, they are also different, with different business models, customers, leases, and sources of revenue.

Speaking of recommendations… here are two ideas for you:

A recent decline presents a nice opportunity in SOLV Energy (MWH). UBS analyst Jon Windham upgrades SOLV to buy from neutral, believing the market is "underappreciating several key tailwinds for SOLV given uncertainty around data center demand and higher interest rates." UBS highlights how the stock has fallen lately despite making two deals and beating and raising recent guidance. UBS says now is an "attractive entry point" for SOLV. (Side note: MWH is a great stock ticker.)

While markets overall fall, BMO remains bullish on Bloom Energy (BE). After speaking with the company, the analyst is upgrading Bloom to outperform, saying he remains "increasingly confident of BE's commercial traction" and that he still has conviction even with the recent drop in shares. While analyst Ameet Thakkar did cut his target to $230 from $260, that's still 50% upside from the current price. Bloom shares have tumbled 45% since October 1.

I was recently asked for my opinion on the price of oil. Here's my take. The market clearly wants to push oil contract prices lower and has for a few months. But, as I've said and reported for a while, don't confuse the futures (paper) price with the actual price. Yes, shipping rates and insurance rates have come down from recent highs — and there is a good amount of oil now flowing through Hormuz — but the cost of a physical barrel of crude is still well above the futures contract. If we get real, longer-lasting peace around Iran, oil prices will fall. There is plenty of oil in the world. That said, if bombs and missiles start firing again, oil prices will firm. I know that's a non-answer answer, but the oil market is truly headline-driven right now. My late good friend Boone Pickens (later in life, he didn't like being called by the "T" in his first name) used to avoid giving a set price target and instead give a directional quote, so I'll do the same: the odds of oil being lower in two months than it is today are 75%.

Last week, I had a one-on-one with Vimal Kapur, chair and CEO of Honeywell International (HON), at an RBC conference in New York City. While the conversation wasn't filmed, I can tell you that Kapur offered investors reassurance that his order book is doing very, very well. This is important because Honeywell is huge in the things that make other things work. Its sensors go into a ton of energy facilities, from refineries to LNG facilities. Honeywell is a leading indicator of spending and buildouts, so if it keeps getting orders, it's a good sign for macro energy.

Bottom line: Watch Honeywell stock as a potential "tell."

This week's Inside Line interview takes a different approach. We hit our friend Craig Johnson up for actionable insights into energy and stocks. Craig is managing director and chief market technician at Piper Sandler. He recently appeared on Power Lunch to talk about markets and stocks. He mentioned being bullish on energy, so we wanted to dig in just a little bit more.

This week, we had the pleasure of chatting with Balaji Krishnamurthy, president of Chevron Australia. We spoke at length about natural gas, the impact of disruptions, and long-term demand from Chevron customers in the Asia-Pacific region.

This is a bit of a local RBI for the New York City area, but one I felt was important to highlight. New York's electric grid operator just came out with a massive warning for everyone in our region: in just a couple of years, there may not be enough electricity supply to power our needs. A new report shows a deficit in seven years, with a nearly 2 GW power hole in a decade. That's a number of big power plants' worth of energy. Get your solar panels on and buy that backup generator! I'll call this one random, but scary.

#2657. Inside the Tape: Jeremiah Trotter Jr. Is Becoming a Cornerstone at LB

Inside the Tape: Jeremiah Trotter Jr. Is Becoming a Cornerstone at LB originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Welcome back to our weekly film series, where we bypass surface-level stats to isolate the schematic tweaks, matchups, and micro-details that define the Eagles from week to week.

After diving into Drew Kendall, Willie Lampkin's first NFL start against the Jaguars in London, and rookie wideout Makai Lemon's expanded opportunity against Dallas on Monday Night weeks ago, the focus today once again returns to Trotter, whose ability to capitalize on opportunities has continued to stand out as the season has progressed.

With Zack Baun sidelined against Pittsburgh, Trotter took over green dot play-calling duties and was absolutely everywhere for 60 minutes. Whether it was attacking gaps in the ground game, finding ways to get a player to the turf in the open field, or showcasing his ability in coverage, every time his number has been called Trotter has found a way to make an impact, and then some.

With a short week ahead as Dallas awaits on Thanksgiving, let's take a closer look at what made Trotter's performance against Pittsburgh pop.

You want to talk about an explosive way to get on the stat sheet early, how about this play on 4th and 1 for your first tackle of the ballgame.

First, one thing that stands out here and throughout the ballgame was the usage of five and six man fronts at times from the Eagles defense. It's not a new thing as they've shown it in weeks prior, but it was a staple on Sunday against a Pittsburgh offense that prides itself on its ability to create movement up front for RB Jaylen Warren. Not in Week 11.

For Trotter, this is textbook linebacker play in a spot where he is initially aligned head up on Steelers center Zach Frazier (54). His ability to not only scrape over a gap, but to the far side of the strength where he ultimately works downhill off the shoulder of TE Pat Freiermuth (88) is an unbelievable showcase of instincts and one-on-tackling ability. There are linebackers in the NFL that could also identify what Trotter saw at the snap, sure, but executing and finishing the play is a whole different ballgame. Having the explosiveness and athleticism to beat RB Rico Dowdle to the spot on a 4th and short opportunity is a play only a select few LBs in football can make. This is outstanding football from Trotter.

See it and attack it. It's something Trotter does on every run play, and it's personified once again here in a violent manner against Warren. At its core, the linebacker position since the dawn of football is a spot where defenders are asked to set the tone. Some guys do it vocally, while Trotter lets his pads do the talking.

This is an Eagles defense that has played some decent football over the last month or so, but there was always more – another level that they could elevate their game to. With Trotter in the middle, the physicality and aggressiveness of the entire front seven moved up a few rungs on the ladder against Pittsburgh.

You all are familiar with the standard Philly has on the defensive side of the ball where the Eagles are expected to dictate terms, not react to them. On Sunday, led by Trotter with pop plays like this, they dominated a Steelers front that has had its way up front for a majority of the 2026 campaign.

I'm not sure Dowdle wants to see Trotter for a long time after this ballgame where No. 54 was consistently able to get the versatile ball-carrier to the turf all afternoon long.

There's good open-field tacklers, and then there's what we've seen from the young Eagles LB all season long. Seemingly, all Trotter needs is to get his hands on a single strand of clothing and guys fall to the turf. It doesn't matter their size or athletic profile, Trotter consistently invents ways to force ball-carriers to the ground – an elite trait at the position.

Making up for the missed tackle from Riq Woolen here, you can only imagine what type of chunk play this could have turned into had Trotter not been able to bring Dowdle to the ground. It's a nice display of his ability to understand ideal pursuit angles, because if Trotter takes the route to work behind Jihaad Campbell (11) who's engaged with Freiermuth (88), Dowdle potentially is able to accelerate and turn that corner before Marcus Epps (29) likely meets him up near the Eagles' 30-yard line, 15 yards from where Trotter ultimately is able to get the Steelers RB to the ground. This is an excellent example of not only covering for a teammate's missed opportunity, but the range and open-field ability to run number-to-number at his position.

This is called a 'spot drop' at the LB position. In zone coverage, you're asked to gain depth quickly where you're not too shallow where QBs can layers throw behind you (and in front of the safety), but also not too deep where it allows YAC (yards after catch) opportunities on designed screen plays underneath for quicker, shiftier athletes in space. It's a great play to rally to the football and to limit any yards after the catch against Freiermuth, but the one thing that stands out here has nothing to do with the play itself.

Watch Trotter's feet… they never stop. For young linebackers, oftentimes in zone coverage you'll see their feet come to a stop when they believe they've reached their necessary depth in coverage. That stops any momentum you have, and in the NFL where there are world class athletes scattered on every depth chart, that extra second it may take to get your lower half going again could be the difference. For Trotter, his feet pitter-pat on and off the turf like a well-oiled piston before quickly accelerating downhill towards the Steelers TE. Footwork is everything in the game of football, no matter the position, and it's the foundation for Trotter in what makes him so impactful in the heart of the Eagles' defense.

#2656. Mike LaFleur: Defense needed a fresh voice

Cardinals head coach Mike LaFleur made a significant change to his coaching staff on Monday.

Defensive coordinator Nick Rallis was dismissed a day after the Cardinals gave up 435 yards in a 34-7 loss to the Chiefs. They lost 34-6 to the Rams the prior week and LaFleur said the team is trying to find solutions for a unit that hasn’t “stopped the bleeding enough.

“I just felt in my heart this morning we needed a change,” LaFleur said, via the team’s website. “A fresh voice.”

LaFleur acknowledged that an offense that’s scored 16 points while starting three different quarterbacks in the last three weeks hasn’t “held our end of the bargain,” but the team has not made any coaching changes on that side of the ball

Senior defensive assistant Teryl Austin will take on the defensive coordinator role on an interim basis. Lafleur said he will “put his own spin” on how the team plays on that side of the ball over the final six games.

#2654. Tennis ball protests could return to the Bundesliga as ultras ramp up pressure on DFB

Less than three years after German football fans successfully fought off the DFL's plans to bring in an external investor, tennis balls could once again be flying onto Bundesliga pitches.

According to a report from BILD, ultra groups across Germany are mobilising ahead of a decisive DFB vote concerning the future of the Regionalliga on 18 December, with almost 100 active fan groups mentioned in the report.

Banners are expected to appear in stadia across the pyramid this weekend, with further demonstrations potentially leading to match interruptions in the weeks that follow.

At the centre of the dispute is the fourth tier of German football, where the current structure of the five regional divisions means that not every champion is guaranteed promotion to the 3. Liga.

Supporters are backing the so-called 'compass model', which would see the Regionalliga reduced to four divisions, with all four champions earning automatic promotion from the 2028/29 campaign.

The proposal, which is also backed by DFB president Bernd Neuendorf, will be put to a vote at next month's extraordinary congress.

An alternative proposal to expand the 3. Liga to 22 teams has met considerable opposition from organised support groups due to the prospect of additional midweek fixtures and the subsequent travelling difficulties for fans.

The coming weeks could therefore see a return of the scenes witnessed across German football in early 2024, when persistent fan protests ultimately contributed to the collapse of the DFL's proposed investor deal

#2653. Sean McVay on Puka Nacua's future with Rams: 'Can't say for certain anything'

Puka Nacua is done for the season, and while the Rams' star receiver is a cornerstone of coach Sean McVay's offense, his future with the franchise is not necessarily a done deal.

Nacua, who had knee surgery last week, is in the final year of his rookie contract.

If he had not been involved in a series of off-the-field incidents dating to 2025, Nacua probably would have had a massive contract extension in hand before this season began.

Instead, the 2025 NFL receptions leader is on track to become a free agent unless he and the Rams agree to an extension, or the Rams place the franchise tag on him or they trade him.

Nacua, a 2023 fifth-round draft pick out of Brigham Young, is earning $5.8 million this season, according to Overthecap.com. He is one of several players who are eligible to become free agents, including defensive lineman Kobie Turner, edge rusher Byron Young and offensive linemen Kevin Dotson, Steve Avila and Warren McClendon Jr., among others.

McVay was asked Monday if he expects Nacua to be with the Rams next season.

"Yeah, we have a lot of guys that are free agents," said McVay, who is preparing his team for Wednesday night's game against the Green Bay Packers. "What I've tried to really do is focus on right here, right now.

"He's obviously a guy that is a very important piece for us for now and for the future, but there are a lot of guys that fall under that category."

Can McVay say for certain that Nacua will be with the Rams in 2027?

"I can't say for certain anything," McVay said. "Here's what I'm not interested in, is something being taken out of context. ... When you go through the amount of free agents that we have, Puka is a huge part of the conversation that you love, but there are also guys that are part of his draft class…

Read more: Plaschke: Without Puka Nacua, the Rams are done

"There are mechanics that we'd like to be able to try to figure out how to keep these really good players and people that we love, and certainly Puka is at the forefront of our mind."

Nacua, 25, is the defendant in a civil lawsuit brought by a woman who alleges he made an antisemitic comment during a New Year's Eve dinner and later bit her while riding in a vehicle. In March, Nacua checked himself into a rehab facility, and in a September interview with Netflix acknowledged alcohol use.

Nacua sat out two games because of a hip injury and played through a calf injury before suffering knee and toe injuries in a Nov. 8 victory over the Washington Commanders. He was placed on injured reserve, had surgery Wednesday, and on Thursday the NFL suspended him three games for violating the league's personal-conduct policy.

"I think he's growing and I trust his heart," McVay said. "I think he's making a lot of steps that are in the right direction."

#2651. Nvidia surge: What investors should do with the stock and the AI trade now

Nvidia shares gapped higher at the open by more than 30%, on pace for its biggest one-day gain in 23 years on Monday, following the news just before the bell that former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang is going to be reinstated.

Investors and traders said that while this reflex rally may have more to run, cautiousness is warranted on Nvidia and the AI trade because of the overhang of investigations into the alleged Chinese espionage involving the company and others, as well as the possibility for data center moratoriums as Democrats take control of Congress and key governor posts in January.

"Am I as bullish on Nvidia now as I was on Friday? Of course not, you can't be," said Dan Niles, founder of Niles Investment Management, which counted Nvidia as its largest position as he bought on the dip over the last few weeks. "I'm trimming back."

"All the same issues that were here before Jensen resigned are still here," he added.

The day before Huang resigned on Nov. 12 as CEO, Nvidia closed at $173.73. It's intraday high on Monday was $171.50, up from its recent low around $125 as it was selling off in the wake controversies. The VanEck Semiconductor ETF closed at $506.96 the day before his resignation. It reached a high of $469.35 before losing steam as trading wore on Monday.

Mike Khouw, YieldMax strategist at Tidal Financial Group, told CNBC on Monday that there were "sighs of relief," "applause" and even "cheering" among investors at the news of Huang's potential reinstatement. But he also said there are still considerable risks facing the AI trade.

Jensen Huang was not the only problem at Nvidia, he said, and there are still many vulnerabilities in the overall AI trade, which should give investors pause. Credit-sensitive companies are still at risk of faltering, Khouw added.

The national security implications of the scandal that has rocked Nvidia and the global AI trade more broadly aren't going away any time soon even as the Trump administration assured the Nvidia board there were none. Investors on Wall Street are expressing strategic concerns about ways to play the tech space just as lawmakers in Washington are demanding answers.

Democrats are demanding answers on what exactly happened with Nvidia and the Chinese government.

"The reckless decision to bring Jensen Huang back as CEO does not change the fact that Mr. Huang, NVIDIA executives, and the company's board still owe Congress answers about the company's compliance with U.S. export controls," Sen. Elizabeth Warren (D-Mass.) said in a Monday statement. "The Trump Administration also needs to explain to Congress what 'input' was given and by which senior officials in the United States government to support his reinstatement."

House Ways and Means ranking member Richard Neal (D-Mass.) stressed the importance of Congressional oversight in determining the best course of action to move forward. The outcome of any Congressional investigations will likely be crucial for Nvidia's long-term viability as an investment opportunity, especially since the company does so much international business.

"This is an export control issue," Neal told CNBC on Monday. "Congress is not subservient to this president or any other president as it relates to trade and tariff policies. This is a trade related issue."

Many of the same Democrats will call for a slowdown in the data center buildout, a strong issue for the public partly responsible for their Blue Wave earlier this month.

"You're going to have to work your way through what these data center moratoriums all mean," said Niles. "Now I think the broader market keeps going but I am not as convinced on the move in the data-center centric names from here."

To be sure, many Wall Street analysts were rushing to upgrade Nvidia's stock on Monday after Huang's reinstatement. Most restored price targets well above $200 with one as high as $270. But many traders think the rebound may fail before getting back to those levels.

Famed short seller Michael Burry, who made a boatload betting against Nvidia with put options as it cratered, told his Substack audience that he thinks the rebound here could be "outsized," especially with low volume in the market overall because of the Thanksgiving holiday this week.

But he too sees the rally eventually losing steam.

"Jensen being back without much more information might even restore NVDA to the high 100s," Burry wrote. "I won't short it on this information I have today until it gets closer to or maybe hurdles $200."

#2650. Cesc Fabregas preaches calm as Como eye historic Champions League push against AEK Athens

Cesc Fabregas has played down expectations ahead of Como's Champions League clash with AEK Athens, insisting his side must stay grounded and stick to their tactical identity. A victory on Tuesday night would move the Italian side significantly closer to securing a dream spot in the knockout stages of Europe's elite competition.

Como are on the verge of a historic achievement as they prepare to host AEK in a pivotal Champions League encounter at the Stadio Sinigaglia. Despite the growing excitement surrounding Como's European adventure, Fabregas is determined to keep his players grounded. The former Arsenal and Chelsea midfielder is focused on the long-term project at the club, refusing to let one competition distract from their overall development and identity on the pitch. Fabregas stressed that his team must be clinical to secure a result against such a physical and dominant side.

"We are not the favorites. We are a small club and we are well aware of how much we can still improve. We have earned the respect we have today, but we must continue to grow calmly and manage our emotions," Fabregas stated, as cited by TUTTOmercatoWEB.

The Spanish tactician has warned his squad to brace for a stern test against the Greek visitors, refusing to underestimate a side that has already proven it can mix it with Europe's elite. Pointing to their impressive pedigree on the continental stage, he noted that AEK have the quality to unsettle even the biggest clubs in the competition. "AEK have even caused problems for teams like Real Madrid. They're a physical, dominant team with a strong mentality. We'll have to be very efficient," Fabregas said.

The manager also drew a parallel to the tactical approach of Massimiliano Alvini, reiterating that his side will not abandon their principles regardless of the scoreline. He insisted that identity comes before everything else, noting that a single result does not define the quality of his squad. "You are not poor when you lose and you are not a phenomenon when you win. There will be matches where we have to suffer, but we will not change our way of playing," he added.

Leading the line for the Italian side is Tasos Douvikas, who currently sits at the top of the Champions League scoring charts. The Greek striker admitted that facing a club from his homeland will make for a poignant evening at the Sinigaglia. Douvikas has been a revelation this season, but he was quick to share the credit with his teammates and the technical staff for his individual success in front of goal.

"It's great to face a Greek team. It will be a special and very emotional match. They're a solid team, with quality and a lot of aggression. The support of our fans will be crucial for us. Being the top scorer is something special, but the credit also goes to the staff and my teammates. I want to continue to grow as a player and as a person," the forward remarked.

With the January transfer window looming, Fabregas was asked if the club's European success might prompt further reinforcements in the market. However, the manager was quick to shut down any speculation, insisting that the focus must remain entirely on the heavy schedule ahead. He pointed out that there are still seven crucial fixtures remaining before the winter break begins, leaving no room for distractions regarding recruitment. "Now's not the time to talk about it, there are still seven games left before the break," he stated.

#2639. Mike McCarthy: Aaron Rodgers is our quarterback

Before an NFL team benches its starting quarterback, someone asks the coach whether the quarterback could be benched.

That happened for the first time during Aaron Rodgers’s two-year tenure with the team on Monday, during a press conference with coach Mike McCarthy, one day after the team’s latest loss, to the Eagles.

It came up as innocuously as possible. McCarthy wasn’t asked whether Rodgers could get the hook; McCarthy was asked whether he’s considering using one of the other three quarterbacks on the roster in an effort to mix things up when the offense is struggling.

“Aaron Rodgers is our quarterback,” McCarthy said.

Of course he is. Even if Rodgers’s play merits benching, Aaron Rodgers isn’t getting benched. It’s implicit in the arrangement.

Still, Aaron Rodgers is a far cry from what he once was. In 10 games this season, Rodgers has completed 220 of 361 passes for 2,241 yards, with 16 touchdown passes and six interceptions. His passer rating of 86.6 is the lowest of his career since becoming Green Bay’s starter in 2008, with the exception of a four-snap performance in 2023 with the Jets (39.6, based on one incomplete pass thrown).

At 6-4, the Steelers remain in contention for another one-and-out playoff appearance. That makes it easier to justify staying the course. With upcoming games against the Broncos, Texans, and Jaguars, that could change.

Even then, Rodgers would never be benched. He’d be “injured.” And his career would end not with a bang but with a whimper.

#2635. NASCAR Driver Makes First Public Appearance After Scary Daytona Crash

Frankie Muniz has made his first public appearance since his scary crash at Daytona International Speedway, showing up at the Las Vegas Grand Prix just 10 days after the incident. The NASCAR driver was spotted in the Formula 1 paddock on Saturday, marking his first documented outing since the November 11 practice crash.

Muniz, who drives the No. 33 Ford for Team Reaume in the NASCAR Craftsman Truck Series, appeared at the Las Vegas Strip Circuit wearing a Ford Racing jacket and cap. He was scheduled to participate in Pirelli Hot Laps following the opening practice session.

His appearance came as he continues to recover from the Daytona accident, which left him sore but without any major injuries. Although he has not announced a return date to racing, the Las Vegas sighting offered a new update on his recovery.

Muniz was involved in a crash on November 11 during practice for the Ford's Proud to Honor Veterans Day Classic at Daytona International Speedway. The exhibition event supported military and veteran charities, and Muniz was scheduled to drive a Mustang Dark Horse R for Team Air Force alongside Austin Cindric.

Muniz crashed in the infield section of the road course. Safety crews had to remove the roof of the car to get him out before transporting him to a local hospital for evaluation. Doctors diagnosed him with soreness, and he was released later that day. He did not compete in the exhibition race.

Following the Daytona crash, Muniz said he was "extremely sore" but had "no major injuries." He thanked Ford Racing, Daytona International Speedway, hospital staff and the safety crews for their help.

"I'm grateful to be walking right now," he said in a video update. "I'll be back on the track soon."

Ford confirmed that Muniz was awake and alert after the crash, while NASCAR also confirmed that he had been released from the hospital. Austin Cindric ultimately won the exhibition race, with a teammate taking Muniz's place in the event.

Muniz had a busy 2026 racing season, competing full-time in the NASCAR Craftsman Truck Series and also racing in the SRO GT4 America series.

He partnered with Tyler Stone to win the GT4 America Am championship, driving a Ford Mustang GT4. His Daytona crash came near the end of an active racing campaign.

The former Malcolm in the Middle star has gradually built a racing career since stepping away from acting. He has made more than 100 Truck Series starts in recent seasons and continues to compete in stock car racing.

His appearance at the Las Vegas Grand Prix does not confirm a return to competition or medical clearance. However, Muniz remains involved with Ford's motorsports activities, having previously taken part in Ford Performance hot laps at the same circuit in 2024 and 2025.

He has not provided a specific timeline for his NASCAR return, and no additional medical details have been released. Fans will be watching for his next update as he prepares for the 2027 racing season.

#2632. The Rams should dominate Packers’ OL

While the Los Angeles Rams' offense is set to be short-handed for the remainder of the season, for at least this week, the defense can lead the way against a weak Green Bay Packers offensive line.

The Rams must win against the Packers. Wednesday night carries significant weight within the NFC playoff picture. Not taking care of business would be a massive blow in a grueling second-half schedule.

Fortunately, Kobie Turner is set to return for the first time since Week 6. He'll join Myles Garrett, Aaron Donald, Byron Young, and Braden Fiske on an aggressive defensive front.

No matter how you split it, Green Bay is inept at protecting its quarterback.

16 big-time throws to six turnover-worthy plays

It's no surprise that quarterbacks play worse when under pressure. Still, this is a stark juxtaposition that one takeaway is clear: Love is a great quarterback when he is kept clean. The Rams have a duty to disrupt him. The Packers must try to protect him against a capable front.

#2631. Josh Kushner and Bob Iger spotted at Lakers-Jazz game

Josh Kushner and Bob Iger attended the Los Angeles Lakers vs. Utah Jazz game alongside Lakers governor Jeanie Buss on Monday, Nov. 23. The trio was seen sitting among the crowd at Crypto.com Arena.

It was the first game they've attended since agreeing in August to purchase the team from Mark Walter at a $12.5 billion valuation. Kushner and Iger have talked with Buss about her role with the organization throughout the sale process.

Kushner also went through the franchise's "back-of-house space" with Rob Pelinka, the Lakers president of basketball operations and general manager, according to ESPN's Dave McMenamin.

Jerry West (#44) played 14 seasons from 1960-1974 with the Lakers and was a force who never left the NBA once he got there, according to USA Today's Jeff Zillgitt. The Hall of Famer, pictured here in action against the Cincinnati Royals, was remembered for his imprint as a shooting guard and builder of teams. His impact remains visible in today’s NBA and he went on to touch generations of teams and players, including LeBron James, who talked to West about winning and losing.

The Lakers often believed that Elgin Baylor (#22) remained under appreciated during his 14-year NBA career with the franchise in Minneapolis and Los Angeles. Baylor, pictured Feb. 17, 1970, in a game against the New York Knicks, is among six Lakers luminaries to have a statue outside of Staples Center, and is also among 10 Lakers players to have their jersey retired. Baylor entered the Naismith Memorial Hall of Fame after winning an NBA championship (1972), posted an NBA Finals record 61 points in Game 5 of the 1961 NBA Finals against Boston, winning the league’s rookie of the year award (1959) and making 11 All-Star appearances.

Los Angeles Lakers center #13 Wilt Chamberlain scored 31,419 points, averaging 30.1 ppg, during 1,045 games over a 16-year Hall of Fame career. Chamberlain led the NBA in scoring seven straight years, averaged 50.4 points per game in 1962 and scored a record 100 points in a single game on March 2, 1962 against the New York Knicks in Hershey, Pennsylvania. The NBA didn’t televise the game and less than 5,000 people got to witness the iconic moment. HoopsHype ranked the top 80 players in NBA history and Chamberlain came in ranked No. 6 on the list, according to Sixers Wire.

Los Angeles Lakers center #33 Kareem Abdul-Jabbar, pictured in action during the 1981-82 season in Indianapolis, is one of the greatest basketball players in history – from his days as a prep star in New York City at Power Memorial Academy to his three NCAA championships and 88-2 record at UCLA and his six NBA championships and six MVPs with the Milwaukee Bucks and Los Angeles Lakers. He averaged at least 21 points in his first 17 seasons, averaged at least 12 rebounds in his first 10 seasons and shot at least 50% from the field.

Los Angeles Lakers guard Magic Johnson (32) in action against the Detroit Pistons during the 1988 NBA Finals at the Silverdome. Magic was the face of the Lakers and the NBA during much of the 1980s when he won five NBA titles and led the "Showtime" teams in legendary showdowns with Larry Bird and the Celtics. The Hall of Famer was a three-time NBA MVP and is regarded as one of the best point guards ever to play the game.

Los Angeles Lakers' Kobe Bryant soars to the basket against Sacramento Kings's Mike Bibby during Game 4 of the NBA Western Conference finals in Los Angeles, May 26, 2002. Kobe spent all 20 years of his legendary career with the Lakers, won five championships, and is the fourth-leading scorer in NBA history. One of the fiercest competitors to play the game, Kobe could do it all. His legend in Lakers Nation will live on forever.

Los Angeles Lakers forward James Worthy celebrates his team's series-clinching victory over the Portland Trail Blazers in Game 6 of the NBA Western Conference Finals at the Great Western Forum in Inglewood, Calif. on May 30, 1991. James Worthy was a cornerstone of the Los Angeles Lakers’ “Showtime” era, helping the franchise win three NBA championships in the 1980s. Known for his clutch performances and smooth all-around game, the Hall of Famer remains one of the most celebrated players in Lakers history.

#2627. ‘Love How Honest Peyton Manning Is About Stefon Diggs Throwing a Tantrum’ — NFL Fans Loved How Broncos Legend Held Commanders WR Accountable

The Washington Commanders pulled out a 22-20 win over the Cincinnati Bengals on Monday, but it was not a quiet night on their sideline. Washington's offense sputtered early, with little passing rhythm and a string of failed third downs, and the frustration boiled over for wide receiver Stefon Diggs.

With the Commanders trailing 3-0 after four straight punts, ESPN cameras caught Diggs shouting, "Hey, throw the [expletive] ball!" Diggs had been targeted just once and had yet to make a catch. The comment appeared to be aimed at quarterback Jayden Daniels, though the footage did not show whom Diggs was addressing.

Either way, Denver Broncos legend Peyton Manning noticed, and he had a stern message for the veteran receiver.

Peyton broke it down on ESPN2's "ManningCast" with his brother Eli. Peyton said Diggs' visible frustration would have the opposite effect on him if he were in Daniels' place.

"If that was me right here, that makes me NOT want to throw him the ball," Manning said. "I go into full, just, BLOCK mode. All you fantasy owners, if that was me playing, see if you could make a trade at halftime, cause I'm not throwing to him at all."

Fans on X enjoyed how directly Manning called Diggs out.

#2626. Lighting error briefly leaves Commanders-Bengals ‘MNF’ game in dark as Roger Goodell looks on

Billy Joel once sang about seeing the lights go down on Broadway, and now football fans can say they saw the lights go down at Northwest Stadium.

The "Monday Night Football" game between the Bengals and Commanders was briefly delayed after the lights went out in the middle of the third quarter just as Jayden Daniels was lining up to hike the ball on third-and-14.

Stadium lights turn off during the third quarter between the Cincinnati Bengals and Washington Commanders at Northwest Stadium. IMAGN IMAGES via Reuters Connect

The ESPN broadcast initially reported that the issue was "power-related and not the stadium" and later added that it had occurred due to "regularly scheduled maintenance for 10 p.m." by the "lighting vendor" that was never disabled.

"It's a weekday thing, and they forgot it was a Monday night game," ESPN broadcaster Joe Buck said on the broadcast.

All the lights in the stadium went out at first, causing the refs to blow the whistle with 11:59 left in the third quarter. After several seconds, the electric signage that wraps around the stadium and the video board came back on, providing some light inside the Landover, Maryland venue.

"Somebody turned out the lights," Buck said.

The telecast showed Bengals quarterback Joe Burrow throwing a football around to keep warm.

NFL commissioner Roger Goodell was also on hand in the suit of Commanders owner Josh Harris.

ESPN threw to commercials after several moments and when the network returned, the lights also returned.

The Bengals were holding a 10-7 lead at the time of the power outage and when play resumed, Commanders quarterback Jayden Daniels was forced to throw the ball away. The Commanders rallied to win the game 22-20 on a walk-off field goal that came with some clock controversey.

The Cincinnati Bengals and the Washington Commanders stand on the field after the lights went off during a game in Landover, Md., Monday, Nov. 23, 2026. Getty Images

NFL commissioner Roger Goodell looks on as the lights go out during Commanders-Bengals. ESPN/X

Washington settled for a field goal that briefly tied the game at 10-10.

Monday's incident is just the latest to occur at the outdated home of the Commanders.

On Jan. 2, 2022, a railing broke at what was then known as FedEx Field sending several football fans tumbling in a near-tragic incident.

Players look on after the lights go out during the third quarter of the game between the Cincinnati Bengals and Washington Commanders at Northwest Stadium. IMAGN IMAGES via Reuters Connect

Raw sewage soaked fans in one section of the stadium during a game against the Chargers after a pipe burst on Sept. 12, 2021.

The Commanders are building a new stadium at the site of the former RFK Stadium in Washington, D.C., that is scheduled to open in 2030.

Speaking with ESPN ahead of the game, Goodell expressed excitement over the new stadium.

"I'm a kid who grew up here. And so I remember going to RFK Stadium. And I think the work that they've done just on the architectural side of it and the construction of it, I think it's going to be remarkable," the NFL commissioner said, according to Front Office Sports.." And having it on the same site, I think it's going to have a wonderful emotional connection. Being in the District is special. And I just think that's going to be a wonderful moment, not only for the league but for the fans."

#2625. Making sense of Liam Coen's nonsensical decision to kick a late field goal

Jacksonville’s loss to the Giants on Sunday included a nonsensical decision with 3:43 to play. Down by eight points and facing fourth and goal from the New York eight, Jaguars coach Liam Coen decided to kick a field goal.

After the game, Jaguars coach Liam Coen justified the decision to take the three points by calling an effort to score a touchdown a “hope play.”

We hope he’s not serious. It was eight yards, not 80. Regardless of what the analytics said, it was time to go for the touchdown, which with a successful two-point conversion would have tied the game.

As explained on Monday’s PFT Live, I think the two-point conversion was the deciding factor for Coen. When the score was 23-9, the Jaguars scored a touchdown that cut the margin to eight. After the touchdown, Coen played the all-too-common analytics game, opting to go for two in the hopes of cutting the margin to six and ideally avoiding overtime if they had scored again.

When it was time to go for two, Coen utilized his best two-point play based on the specific matchup of the Jacksonville offense against the New York defense. It failed. Which meant that, if/when the Jaguars scored another touchdown, they would need to rely on Coen’s second-best two-point play.

After Plan A failed, why would Coen believe Plan B would succeed?

And so instead of going for a touchdown which may or may not have been capped by a successful two-point try, Coen opted to take the three and kick off. Which pissed off the New York offense, and which helped set the stage for the Jaguars never getting the ball again.

If the Jaguars had gone for the score, the Giants would have gotten the ball deep in their own end. The Jaguars would have been likely to get another shot at a touchdown and a two-point conversion.

Still, the Jaguars would have had to convert a two-point play after their best option at going for two had failed. In the moment, that reality may have been the difference for Coen.

Which brings us back to the analytics-driven trend to go for two after scoring a touchdown when trailing by 14. If the two-pointer fails, the coach needs to be confident in the second-best two-point play for the week. Coen likely wasn’t, which contributed to the decision to not try to gain eight yards in a one-score game.

That’s the most important factor when it comes to relying on math. Once teams start down the go-for-two path, their confidence (or lack thereof) in the potential for successful two-point plays becomes critical.

Coen’s best two-point play failed. Which may have shaken his faith in his second-best two-point play. Which may have prompted him to opt for the three points in lieu of rolling the dice on scoring from the eight — before rolling the dice on scoring again from the two.

Mychal Thompson, left, and Michael Cooper honor the late Lawrence Tanter during a game between the Lakers and Jazz on Monday, Nov. 23, 2026, in Los Angeles. Getty Images

"As much as you guys love him, we love him even more," said Thompson, who won two championships with the Lakers in 1987 and 1988 as Tanter served as the team's P.A. announcer.

The Los Angeles Lakers honor Lawrence Tanter outside Crypto.Com Arena on Monday, Nov. 23, 2026, in Los Angeles, before a game against the Utah Jazz. NBAE via Getty Images
