#2620. Commanders QB Jayden Daniels hits a pair of big passes to set up game-winning field goal to beat Bengals

It looked like Joe Burrow might have added to his MVP résumé by leading a game-winning drive. But Jayden Daniels got the last word.

After the Cincinnati Bengals kicked a field goal with 46 seconds left to take a 20-19 lead, Daniels hit two huge passes downfield to get the Washington Commanders in range for a short field goal and the win. Drew Stevens made the kick and Washington escaped with a 22-20 win.

The Bengals had a shot to end the game but couldn't run more time off the clock after getting in field-goal range. That allowed Daniels a shot. He hit rookie Antonio Williams for 22 yards and then 42 yards to set up the win on Stevens' 26-yard field goal.

Burrow had taken over as the MVP favorite before Monday night's game. But Daniels has had moments as a special quarterback, too, and he and Williams had a magical couple of plays to pull out a dramatic win.

The game didn't start with the Bengals tearing up a defense, as has been the case in recent weeks. The game was scoreless after the first quarter, mostly due to rain. That affected both offenses, though the weather cleared up and both teams started to move the ball better.

The Bengals took a 10-0 lead on a great catch in the end zone by Tee Higgins for a 31-yard score.

The Commanders had their protection issues in the first half, most notably blocking Boye Mafe who had three sacks before halftime. But a long drive near the end of the first half resulted in a Jacory Croskey-Merritt touchdown, and early in the first half a 47-yard run by Daniels set up a game-tying field goal.

The Bengals had been one of the hottest offenses in the NFL but the Commanders did a good job keeping pressure on Burrow and containing him, with an assist from the weather early in the game.

The Bengals got a touchdown to take the lead in the third quarter, as Burrow hit rookie Colbie Young for a 4-yard score after the QB was chased out of the pocket. But the Bengals couldn't do much else for most of the second half.

Part of the problem is they can't run the ball, and have given up trying. The Commanders had a good plan against a pass-heavy offense and the Bengals didn't have a counter-punch. That could end up being a problem for Cincinnati in the playoffs.

The Commanders weren't doing much themselves on offense, but had a couple field goals to keep them in the game. Then came a long drive at the right time. Highlighted by Stefon Diggs' 29-yard catch on second-and-20, the Commanders chewed up a lot of clock and took a lead. The Commanders had a big decision, whether to go for it on fourth-and-1 with less than two minutes left. Washington kicked the field goal for the 19-17 lead, but that left Burrow 2:26 to get in field-goal range and retake the lead.

Burrow got the drive going with a 12-yard pass to Higgins, and then he hit tight end Mike Gesicki for 32 yards. That put the Bengals in field-goal range and led to the Commanders starting to call their timeouts. There was almost a game-ending mistake from Burrow, but rookie linebacker Sonny Styles had a pass go off his hands and it was incomplete rather than an interception. Then Burrow took a sack, which was a loss of eight yards and made the field goal harder. McPherson came on for a 56-yard attempt and he nailed it. At that point it seemed like Cincinnati would escape.

Daniels had 46 seconds to answer. And he did, going to the middle of the field twice for Williams for big gains. Williams told ESPN afterward that the Commanders ran the same play for both catches.

The win helped the Commanders improve to 4-5-1. Washington still has a ways to go to get back in the NFC East race, but Daniels will do his best to keep the Commanders in it.

Antonio Williams finished the night with 9 catches for 122 yards, including 64 on Washington's game-winning drive.

Drew Stevens connected on all five of his field-goal attempts, including a walk-off winner.

And Jayden Daniels delivered when it mattered most. He finished the night completing 23 of 35 passes for 289 yards while adding 55 yards on the ground, including a 47-yard run that set up a Washington score.

#2617. Wolves 142, Nets 116: Defense Optional, Vibes Exceptional

Well, that was fun. If beatdowns and revenge against a former coworker are your thing, tonight's matchup was for you.

If they aren't, well, this was still a fun one.

On Monday night, the Minnesota Timberwolves dispatched the Brooklyn Nets 142-116. Minnesota did what it was supposed to do against one of the worst teams in the league as the Wolves look to put the harrowing memories of the Chicago Bulls game to rest.

Defense was clearly optional in this one, as both teams shot greater than 50% from the floor and over 40% from deep. Fortunately for us, what little defensive presence that did exist in this game came from the Timberwolves. Rudy Gobert and Joan Beringer had four steals apiece, and Beringer capped off his defensive showing with a whopping four blocks. All in all, this was one of the most complete showings that the Timberwolves have had all season.

With Thanksgiving around the corner, I'd like to take this opportunity to remind everyone that we, as Wolves fans, should be thankful that this team is good enough to consistently and authoritatively put away bad teams. Being at home for eight straight games surely helps team morale and rest, though this was a game that should have been, and was, definitively won. Not much room to poke holes in this performance. Even Rudy Gobert had a nice game!

While the Nets made a couple of runs in the second and fourth quarters to keep the game mildly interesting, there was never a sense of danger for the Wolves. They handled their business and look forward to the next matchup. Onward!

Here are a few of my takeaways from the game:

I'm not sure Anthony Edwards's prophetic line about how "we got Jaden McDaniels" is wholly accurate anymore. We might just have Kevin Durant. What a scintillating performance.

Already averaging career highs across all but one counting stat (blocks), McDaniels looks like there are no weak points left in his game. His midrange shots feel automatic. His threes are going in at a consistent clip amidst a steady volume of just over four attempts per game. Tonight, he made all four of his shots from distance, shot 12/16 from the floor as a whole, and set a new career high in points with 33 big ones.

Cap his performance off with 10 boards and six assists, and we might be looking at one of the best games of his career.

Most Wolves coverage this year has focused its attention on LaMelo Ball, Jonathan Kuminga, and the zany foibles of Gobert. In a sense, the silence around McDaniels speaks to his consistency and the greater expectations that the fanbase has for him. He's coming to work and doing his job every single night.

To say McDaniels might be the most important member of the team in the absence of Edwards is not an understatement. It certainly doesn't hurt that Jaden looks like Kevin Durant when he's playing, too. Silky. Smooth.

Ball has been in a statistical slump over the last few games – that much can't be argued. Tonight's performance was not much better in that regard, as he shot 6/16 from the floor, 2/10 from three, and only compiled three assists. At face value, this looks like a pedestrian performance.

Yet, I remain convinced that he is one of the most impactful players that the Timberwolves have on their roster. The highlights and deep shots do not necessarily tell the entire story of who he is as a player and how he impacts the game. What I'm saying is: we need to look beyond the box score to measure his effectiveness.

Yeah, I'm going to make a comparison to Rudy Gobert.

In the same vein as how Gobert's defensive impact cannot always be measured by things that show up on the stat sheet, Ball has that same effect on the offensive end. He is a master at manipulating defenses and drawing attention. His court vision is elite enough to make the right decision almost every time, and more often than not, he is putting his teammates in position to either make a play for themselves or to make a play for someone else.

Of course, hockey assists and other things like that are not counted in basketball. This is, however, why the Charlotte Hornets had such a crazy offensive rating last season when Ball stayed healthy. When he's on the court, everybody around him is better. It's not a coincidence. He carries all of the benefits of a heliocentric player with so few of the risks.

The shots will drop eventually. They may not be consistent, but that's okay! You live and die by those moments until Edwards returns. Until then, continue to appreciate everything that Ball has been offering this team in terms of flow, pace, and vision.

The sentiment of "they're all bad defenders" is no longer reserved for the Denver Nuggets. These Nets players have no defensive identity, or any identity at all.

It should come as no surprise that a team that lacks defensive intensity is one that is helmed by Julius Randle. Look, I get it, he did some great things during his time in Minnesota. He was an integral part of a team that made the Western Conference Finals. Randle had some great series, especially some standout ones on defense against the Los Angeles Lakers and Golden State Warriors, but that's about as far as any praise for defensive effort can go in his direction.

Randle had a great offensive showing tonight with 29 points on 11/16 from the floor. He also had his trademark five turnovers and finished with a team-worst -25 across his 35 minutes of play.

To be fair, the Nets are not a franchise that needs to be winning just yet. Still, when you have guys like Moritz Wagner blowing kisses to the away crowd while down by 20+ points, it's indicative of a team culture that does not take the game seriously. That comes from the top.

I do not think there should be any ill will towards Randle, though the resurgent team culture of the Timberwolves under LaMelo's leadership is exactly what the doctor ordered. Randle will get his stats while the Wolves accrue happiness points and wins. Seems like a good deal to me.

The Timberwolves take on the skidding Oklahoma City Thunder on Wednesday night at 6:30 PM. Catch that matchup on DAZN-MN and ESPN.

#2613. Game Recap: Suns defeat Pelicans 123-119 in OT for fourth straight road win

The Phoenix Suns played their first overtime game of the season, as their battle in the Big Easy was anything but. They walked away with a 123-119 victory over the Pelicans, surviving a Jordan Poole variance magnet and extending their winning streak to four games, all of which have come on this road trip. It's also the sixth consecutive victory for Phoenix over New Orleans.

The Pelicans' bench provided quite the challenge, scoring 67 points and stealing the narrative the Suns typically own with the production of their second unit. Three New Orleans reserves scored in double figures, with Jordan Poole leading the way with 28 points on 9-of-12 shooting, including 6-of-8 from beyond the arc. Zion Williamson was the only Pelicans starter to reach double figures, scoring 25 points.

Meanwhile, four Suns starters scored in double figures, with Collin Gillespie providing 18 points off the bench on 7-of-11 shooting, including 3-of-6 from deep. He played the entire fourth quarter and overtime after exiting the game earlier following a scary sequence in which Ryan Dunn appeared to roll up on his leg.

It was a tight game throughout, featuring 19 lead changes and 12 ties. Ultimately, the Suns pulled it out in overtime, fueled by five points from Gillespie and four from Miles Bridges.

The Suns improve to 4-0 on their current road trip and will look to complete a clean sweep when they wrap things up Wednesday night in Houston.

Jalen Green was the offensive catalyst early for Phoenix, at least from a shot volume perspective. He accounted for four of the Suns' first seven field goal attempts, connecting on two of them.

Meanwhile, the Pelicans employed a strategy we've seen plenty of times before. Give Zion Williamson the ball on the perimeter, let him get downhill and attack the basket, drawing fouls and creating and-one opportunities along the way.

The Pelicans aren't a particularly good rebounding team, and neither are the Suns. In the first five minutes alone, Phoenix surrendered five offensive rebounds, which New Orleans converted into five second-chance points.

The first round of substitutions included Khaman Maluach, who immediately made his presence felt on the glass and recorded an impressive block on the physical Zion Williamson. His insertion into the game helped spark a 13-3 run for the Suns.

Ultimately, it was the New Orleans second unit that brought the most energy in the opening quarter. The Pelicans' bench accounted for 22 of their 35 first-quarter points, fueling a 14-3 run in response to Phoenix's earlier surge, including a buzzer-beating three by Jordan Poole to end the quarter.

The Pelicans carried that energy into the second quarter, playing with more physicality and pushing the pace, which was to be expected from a team that ranks in the top five in the NBA in pace.

Jordan Poole was feeling it, and I think we all know what happens when Jordan Poole starts feeling it. Either grab the fire extinguisher or body him up. Poole started his night 6-of-6 from the field for 17 points, including 4-of-4 from beyond the arc.

Still, the Suns had an answer for everything the Pelicans threw at them during Poole's heater. Halfway through the second quarter, Phoenix found itself with a 48-47 lead.

With 3:28 remaining in the quarter, Yves Missi went up for a dunk, and Khaman Maluach elevated to deny him. A foul was called on Maluach, prompting Jordan Ott to challenge the play. The challenge was successful, overturning the call to an offensive foul on Missi.

The minutes Maluach provided in the first half were spectacular. He was playing with physicality, and his hands have been looking much better as of late. Twice the Suns penetrated the defense and found him in the paint. Twice he caught it. Twice he dunked it.

The Suns outscored the Pelicans 25-19 in the second quarter. New Orleans shot 8-of-16 from deep in the first half, but their 11 turnovers allowed Phoenix to hang around.

The Suns surrendered only three more offensive rebounds than they collected and were outrebounded by two overall, although it certainly felt much different watching the game unfold.

Jordan Poole led all scorers with 17 points off the bench, while Dillon Brooks and Jalen Green each had 10 points. Khaman Maluach contributed six points and five rebounds in 10 minutes of action.

Heading into halftime, the Suns held a 56-54 lead.

The Suns opened the second half on a 5-0 run, and Pelicans head coach Jamahl Mosley opted to use a challenge after Herb Jones was called for fouling Dillon Brooks on a jump shot. The challenge was unsuccessful, although Brooks proceeded to miss both free throws.

Shortly thereafter, with the Suns leading 61-58, Jordan Ott utilized his second challenge of the night, disputing whether the ball went out of bounds off Jeremiah Fears following a Devin Booker block. The challenge was successful, and Phoenix retained possession. Both teams, however, were now out of challenges.

The third quarter was a back-and-forth affair, but one of the more painful moments came following a collision between Ryan Dunn and Collin Gillespie as they pursued a rebound after a missed transition opportunity by Zion Williamson.

Dunn was pushed into Gillespie, taking his legs out from underneath him. Gillespie exited the game and immediately headed to the locker room.

Jordan Poole returned to the game and continued to cook. After falling behind by seven points, the Pelicans rattled off a 16-2 run that included another Poole three-pointer, this one coming with a foul that was upgraded to a Flagrant 1.

It certainly didn't help that during a 10-0 stretch of that run, Jalen Green opted to go into isolation and chuck up an airball from beyond the arc.

The Pelicans outscored the Suns 29-23 in the third quarter, fueled by another 11 points off the bench from Jordan Poole and eight from Zion Williamson. Devin Booker scored 10 points in the quarter, but Phoenix trailed 83-79 heading into the fourth.

The fourth quarter started with a welcome site: Gillespie returned to the floor.

We once again saw a lineup featuring Dillon Brooks alongside the second unit, although Oso Ighodaro was out there as well. The Suns quickly tied the game, but the Pelicans continued to use their size to their advantage, continually attacking the interior and getting to the line. Derik Queen benefitted from this aggression, shooting 5 free throws through the halfway mark of the fourth. He hit one.

The Suns, after being down by as much as 6, tied the game on an impressive Jalen Green 13-footer and took a 100-98 lead on a Miles Bridges reverse. Housekeeping note: Upon review, a Luke Kennard three-pointer was ruled a two-pointer, so it was a one-point lead for Phoenix, 99-98.

Jalen Green threw down a beautiful transition dunk and buried a three to put Phoenix up 104-100. Then came the carelessness. Green tried to do too much, Bridges followed with a poor pass, and consecutive Suns turnovers fueled a quick 6-0 Pelicans run.

The Suns regained the lead courtesy of a Miles Bridges free throw and a Collin Gillespie layup. Saddiq Bey tied it at 107 with 46.3 seconds remaining. The next possession? A patented Devin Booker turnaround 13-footer. Splash. Jeremiah Fears tied it at 109 with 22.8 seconds left. Booker passed out of a double team, got the ball back, and had his pass intended for Gillespie intercepted by Herb Jones. The Suns never got a shot off. Jones missed his half-court heave.

The unit out there to start overtime? Collin Gillespie, Jalen Green, Devin Booker, Miles Bridges, and Dillon Brooks. Yes, the Suns were going small. On the opening defensive possession, Gillespie did a stellar job of staying in front of Zion Williamson, forcing him into a fadeaway rather than allowing him to get to the rim.

He then converted an and-one opportunity on the other end to give Phoenix a three-point lead. Miles Bridges canned a three-pointer on the ensuing Suns possession, extending the lead to four.

The Suns looked much more locked in defensively during overtime. Their closeouts were spectacular, and it felt like they were everywhere. Even though they continued to get owned on the offensive glass, the defensive intensity was there, highlighted by a possession in which Devin Booker ripped the ball away from Zion Williamson.

Gillespie then knocked down a 19-footer as part of a 10-2 Phoenix run in overtime to give Phoenix the 6-point lead.

New Orleans continued to chip away as overtime progressed, and Saddiq Bey threw up a Hail Mary three-pointer on a play in which it appeared he traveled, pulling the Pelicans within three points with 25 seconds remaining.

The Suns inbounded the ball, and New Orleans began playing the foul game, sending Miles Bridges to the line. He once again went 1-of-2, giving Phoenix a four-point lead. The Pelicans chucked up another three, missed, and fouled the Suns with 2.6 seconds remaining.

Phoenix secured its fourth consecutive road victory, defeating New Orleans 123-119.

The road trip ends on Wednesday night with a Thanksgiving Eve battle against the Rockets in Houston.

#2612. Vikings Football and the Fine Art of Catastrophizing

In Week 11, Minnesota Vikings fans waited all day for Sunday Night. . .and almost immediately wished that they hadn't.

In what had been billed as one of the games of the year in the National Football League, the Vikings managed to hang with the San Francisco 49ers for about a quarter and a half before a late second-quarter touchdown pass from Brock Purdy and a pick-six from Kirk Cousins essentially ended things before halftime. The scoreboard said 35-10 at the end of the night, but it was over long before that.

Of course, the internet being what it is, there was much weeping and gnashing of teeth and rending of garments and those sorts of things. The euphoria that had washed over Vikings fans following the return of Kirk-a-Mania just a week prior had been swept away and replaced with the lament that Brian Flores' defense had been "exposed," and that teams around the league now had the "blueprint" to beat the league's top defense going forward.

Because that's the way things are now. Everything is either the greatest or the worst. Hell, sometimes things are both of those things simultaneously, somehow. Don't ask me how it happens, because I don't know. I'm fifty years old, and everything hurts. . .I can barely walk and chew gum at the same time, let alone reconcile those two things simultaneously.

But yes, the Niners put forth a very specific blueprint on Sunday night on how to beat the Vikings' defense. Step one of that blueprint is, apparently, "Start Brock Purdy at quarterback." That eliminates a lot of other teams from being able to emulate it. Honestly, that guy is operating on a level that pretty much no other quarterback in the league is currently operating on. I'll listen to an argument for Joe Burrow, but at this point that's just about it. He handled Flores' scheme in a way that nobody has in a very long time.

But even if the Niners did put a "blueprint" out there. . .so what?

Let me illustrate this with a slightly different example. If you play video games and you get stuck on a specific part of the game, you can pause the game or turn it off, head on over to YouTube, and find no shortage of videos of people showing you everything you need to do in order to get past that one particular spot you're having trouble with. You can probably even find four or five different people who will show you four or five different strategies to get through exactly the same map or boss or whatever it might be. You can watch all the videos your eyes can handle.

Eventually, however, you reach a point where you have to step away from the video, go back to your console, pick up your controller, and do the job yourself. And if your reflexes aren't fast enough or you hit the wrong button at the wrong time. . .well, that "blueprint" that you got from watching all those videos really doesn't do you a whole lot of good, does it? Teams can know what they have to do in order to beat the Vikings' defense all they want. How many teams can actually go out and do it?

The Vikings have lost three games so far this season. In two of those three losses, they were competitive until the late stages of the game, despite Kyler Murray and Carson Wentz looking like they had never actually seen a football before, never mind having any idea what the heck they were actually supposed to do with one. In the other one, they happened to run into the one team equipped. . .almost uniquely so. . .to beat them at what they do best. And if the Vikings should happen to meet up with that team again at some point, Flores has earned enough of my trust at this point for me to believe he's going to make enough changes to not let it happen again.

Now. . .while I can understand the disappointment after watching a game like Sunday night's loss, there's plenty of reason to be positive about the rest of the season. Am I crazy? Possibly, but that has nothing to do with football. Allow me to explain what I'm talking about as far as the Vikings are concerned.

The Vikings have seven games left on the schedule. Only two of those games will be played away from U.S. Bank Stadium. Those will be the Thursday Night Football game in Week 14 at New England and Week 17 at the New York Jets. The Patriots will be without Drake Maye and, as of now, are starting Tommy "In a Gadda" Devito at quarterback. The Jets. . .well, I was always taught that if you can't say anything nice. . .

The Vikings also only have two games remaining against teams with winning records the rest of the way. Both of them are against division opponents, and both of them are at home. Those will be on Sunday Night Football in Week 15 against Detroit (coming off a mini-bye following the New England game), and the regular-season finale against Chicago. The other three games are against Atlanta (currently 3-7), Carolina (currently 5-5), and Washington (currently 4-5-1).

According to PlayoffStatus.com, despite currently sitting behind both Detroit and Chicago in the NFC North, the Vikings have the inside track to both the NFC North title and the #2 seed in the NFC playoffs. They also have the second-highest odds of making the playoffs overall in the NFC at 93%. According to Tankathon, in terms of remaining strength of schedule, the Bears, Lions, and Packers rank #1, #5, and #7, respectively. The Vikings rank. . .#16. So the sledding is much tougher for the rest of the division, at least on paper, than it is for the purple.

You can make a pretty convincing argument that the Vikings could (should?) be favored in every game they play from here on out. The Lions and Bears play each other on Thanksgiving, so barring a tie, one of them is going to come out of Week 12 with a third loss. The Vikings have swept the Packers already, so Green Bay would need a minor miracle to overtake Minnesota for anything at this point.

The bottom line is that there's still plenty of football to be played, and while, yes, the loss to the Niners was painful to watch, it's not going to ruin the season or anything. There are adjustments to be made and, hopefully, this team will make them. Now it's time for them. . .and for us, as fans. . .to get up, dust ourselves off, and get ready for the last seven games of the year, starting with Kirkomecoming (if that's even a word, and if it's not, I just made it up) on Sunday afternoon at U.S. Bank Stadium.
